Optimal Stateless Model Checking of Transactional Programs under Causal Consistency DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX Conference: ACM Symposium on Principles of Distributed Computing; June 19–23, 2023; Orlando, FL Price: 15.00 ISBN: 978-1-4503-XXXX-X/18/06
Parosh Aziz Abdulla, Mohamed Faouzi Atig, Ashutosh Gupta, Shankaranarayanan Krishna, Omkar Tuppe

Abstract
. We present a framework for efficient stateless model checking (SMC) of concurrent programs under five prominent models of causal consistency, 𝙲𝙲𝚟 , 𝙲𝙼 , 𝙲𝙲 , 𝚁𝙰𝚝 , 𝚁𝙲𝚘𝚖 {\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt CC},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom} . Our approach is based on exploring traces under the program order p ​ o {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po} and the reads from 𝑟𝑓 {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}} relations. Our SMC algorithm is provably optimal in the sense that it explores each p ​ o {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po} and 𝑟𝑓 {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}} relation exactly once. We have implemented our framework in a tool called Tranchecker . Experiments show that Tranchecker performs well in detecting anamolies in classical distributed databases benchmarks.



1.
Introduction

Transactions are a proven abstraction mechanism in database systems
for constructing reusable parallel computations.
A transactional program is a concurrent program, which has
transactions
in the code of its processes. Transactional programming
(
Harris
et al. 2010
)
attempts to adapt the powerful transaction processing technology originally developed for database concurrency, to
the setting of general purpose concurrent programs. Using transactions as the main enabling construct
for shared memory concurrency obliviates the programmer
from using low level mechanisms like locks, mutexes and semaphores to prevent two concurrent threads from interfering.

A
transaction
is a
code segment consisting of several instructions which must be executed
atomically
. Each process in a transactional program is a sequence of transactions,
written as
\mathtt{t1}[\dots];\mathtt{t2}[\dots];\dots;\mathtt{tn}[\dots]
, where
\mathtt{t}[\dots]
represents a
transaction, with the brackets [ and ] demarcating the beginning and end. The general idea is that each process
p
executes its transactions following the program order, and, when a process
p
finishes executing a transaction
\mathtt{t}[\dots]
, it “delivers” the writes/data updates made in

\mathtt{t}[\dots]
to all processes. The manner in which the receiving processes make use of this delivery is dependent on the underlying consistency model of transactional programs.

The strongest consistency model is the one ensuring serializability
(
Papadimitriou 1979
)
, where every execution of a program is equivalent
to another one where the transactions are executed serially one after the other, with no interference. In the non-transactional setting,
this model corresponds to sequential consistency (SC)
(
Lamport 1979
)
. While serializability and
SC are easy to understand for programmers, they are too strong, since they
need global synchronization between all processes, which makes it difficult to achieve good performance guarantees

(
Fischer
et al. 1985
;
Gilbert and Lynch 2002
)
. Thus, modern databases
ensure weaker consistency guarantees.
Causal consistency
(
Lamport 1978
)
, is one of the fundamental models implemented in many leading production databases such as
AntidoteDB, CockroachDB and MongoDB. Unlike serializability, causal consistency is a weaker concurrency notion
and allows conflicting transactions having reads/writes to a common variable to be executed in different orders by different processes as long as they are not causally related. The sets of updates visible to different processes may differ and read actions may return values that cannot be obtained in SC executions. There are many variations of causal consistency introduced in the literature, some of the prominent ones being weak causal consistency (
CC
)
(
Perrin
et al. 2016
;
Bouajjani et al. 2017a
)
, causal memory (
CM
)
(
Ahamad et al. 1995
;
Perrin
et al. 2016
)
, causal convergence (
CCv
)
(
Burckhardt 2014
)
, read atomicity (
{\tt RAt}
)
(
Cerone
et al. 2015
)
,
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
as well as read committed (
{\tt RCom}
)
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
,
(
Berenson et al. 1995a
)
.

In a recent survey

(
Pavlo 2017
)
of database administrators, more than 85% of the participants responded that almost all transactions in their database execute at these weak levels of concurrency. A weaker concurrency notion allows more behaviours than stronger notions. The onus of ensuring that a database application
can tolerate this larger set of behaviours is on the developers. These weaker models, while being deployed in most distributed databases, are hard to reason about
(
Brutschy et al. 2017
)
. The resulting application bugs can cause huge losses
(
Warszawski and
Bailis 2017
)
. Thus, there is a tradeoff between good performance as in the weak models versus correctness as in serializability. One can easily reason about database applications under serializability; however doing this for the weak models is challenging since they have exponentially more behaviours. Given the
widespread use of weak models in modern distributed databases, we need verification techniques which ensure that applications are not only
efficient but also correct.

Verification of Database Applications
. This paper focuses on verifying the correctness of concurrent programs under causal consistency against assertion violations. Model checking
(
Clarke
et al. 1983
)
is the most prominent technique for algorithmic verification of programs which explores systematically, all executions of a program. The limiting factor in the applicability of model checking is state space explosion; the number of executions grows exponentially in the number of processes.

Stateless model checking (SMC), one of the most
successful techniques for finding concurrency bugs
(
Godefroid 1997
)
is useful to counter this state space explosion. The name “stateless" model checking comes from the fact that only a small fraction of the visited states are stored when exploring executions. While this avoids excessive memory consumption to some extent, we still need to cope up with the large amount of non-determinism which gives rise to exponentially many interleavings.
To address this, SMC is often combined with Partial Order reduction (POR). POR
(
Clarke
et al. 1999
;
Godefroid 1996
;
Peled 1993
)

is a technique that limits the number of executions explored without compromising on the coverage of program behaviour. We achieve this
by avoiding the analysis of equivalent executions.
In POR, two executions are considered equivalent if one can be obtained from the other by swapping consecutive independent execution steps. In POR, all the executions are partitioned among equivalence classes, and at least one execution from each representative equivalence class is explored. Dynamic partial order reduction (DPOR), which combines
SMC and POR,
explores
the executions by computing the equivalence between executions on-the-fly.

DPOR was first developed for concurrent programs under SC
(
Abdulla
et al. 2014
;
Sen and Agha 2006
)
.
Recent years have seen DPOR adapted to language induced weak memory models
(
Kokologiannakis et al. 2018
;
Norris and Demsky 2016
;
Abdulla
et al. 2018
)
, as well as hardware-induced relaxed memory models
(
Abdulla et al. 2015
;
Zhang
et al. 2015
)
.
Under sequential consistency, the equivalence classes are called
Mazurkiewicz
traces
(
Mazurkiewicz 1987
)
, while for relaxed memory models, the generalization of these are called
Shasha-Snir
traces
(
Shasha and Snir 1988
)
. A Shasha-Snir trace characterizes an execution of a concurrent program by the relations (1)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
program order, which totally orders events of each process, (2),
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
reads from, which connects each read with the write it reads from, (3)
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}co}
coherence order, which totally orders writes to the same shared variable. DPOR can be optimized further by observing that the assertions to be verified at the end of an execution does not depend on the coherence order of shared variables, and hence it suffices to consider traces over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
. Based on this observation, the DPOR algorithms for programs under the release-acquire semantics (RA) and SC

(
Abdulla
et al. 2018
)
,
(
Abdulla et al. 2019
)
explores traces with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}co}
where the
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}co}
edges are added on the fly.
The equivalence classes are considered wrt
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
, reducing
the number of distinct traces to be analyzed.

Since a program may behave entirely differently when run on different consistency models, verification frameworks such as DPOR need to be resigned from one model to the next.
In particular, it is not possible to migrate existing DPOR algorithms for models such as SC and RA
to causal consistency.
The crucial challenge in the design of such an algorithm is (i) to compute the set the write events from which
read events can fetch their values, and (ii) calculate the causalities that such a read operation implies.
These operations are intricately dependent on the consistency model.
For instance, they have an exponential cost in SC, but only a polynomial cost in RA
(
Abdulla et al. 2019
;
Abdulla
et al. 2018
)
.
This paper aims to develop DPOR algorithms for transactional programs under causal consistency.
To that end, we face two obstacles, namely that (i) we are dealing with new consistency models (compared to existing DPOR algorithms),
and (ii) we consider transactions (rather than simple transitions).
Concretely, we
determine efficiently, on-the-fly, which transaction to read from whenever we encounter a read
instruction. Whether a transaction is “readable” depends on the individual model under consideration, which is different for all of them. Second, each time we read from one of the readable transactions, specific causal
dependencies are created amongst the others. These must be resolved so that the resultant execution remains
consistent concerning the model.
We instantiate our framework for the five variants

{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
. Despite this heterogeneity, we propose a uniform DPOR algorithm across all five models, which computes the readable set of transactions and resolves causal dependencies in polynomial time.

Contributions
. We propose a DPOR based SMC algorithm for the models
X{\in}\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}\}
which explores systematically, all the distinct
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
traces covering all possible executions of the program.
We develop a uniform algorithm for all models which is sound and complete : that is, all traces explored are consistent wrt the model
X
under consideration, and all such consistent traces are explored.
Moreover, our algorithm is
optimal
in the sense that, each consistent
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
trace is explored
exactly once. One of the key challenges during the trace exploration is to maintain the consistency of the traces wrt the model under consideration. We tackle this by defining a
trace semantics

which ensures that the traces generated in each step only contain edges which will be present in any consistent trace. We implement our algorithms in a tool
Tranchecker
which is, to the best of our knowledge, the first of its kind to perform SMC on transactional programs wrt these five prominent causal consistency models.
Tranchecker
checks for assertion violation of programs under
{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
.

We generated 7507 litmus tests using Herd
(
Alglave
et al. 2014
)

and evaluate
Tranchecker
on them.
Tranchecker
takes around
570s to finish running all 7507 programs.
Then we proceed with experimental evaluation on a wide range of benchmarks from distributed databases.
We showed that (i)
Tranchecker
correctly detects
known consistency bugs
(
Bernardi and
Gotsman 2016
)

,
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
,
(
Beillahi
et al. 2021b
)
and

(
Beillahi
et al. 2021a
)

under
{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt CC},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom}
,
(ii)
Tranchecker
correctly detects known assertion
violations in applications
(
Difallah et al. 2013
)
,
(
Holt et al. 2016
)
,
(
Beillahi
et al. 2021b
)
,
(
Nair
et al. 2020
)
.
We also did a stress test of
Tranchecker
on some
parameterized benchmarks which
resulted in a large number (7.7 million) of traces.

Related Work
.
We compare our contribution with two lines of work. On one hand,
we review work on the verification of causal
consistency models, and on the other hand, on
SMC algorithms.

There has been a spate of recent work on causal consistency models

(
Ahamad et al. 1995
)
. The question of verifying automatically, whether a given transactional program is robust against causal consistency has been explored in many papers,

(
Bernardi and
Gotsman 2016
)
,
(
Brutschy et al. 2017
)
,
(
Brutschy et al. 2018
)
,

(
Cerone and
Gotsman 2018
)
,

(
Nagar and
Jagannathan 2018
)
via under and over approximation
techniques. Checking robustness amounts to asking whether the behaviours obtained while executing over a causally consistent database are serializable. The latest in this
direction providing a precise robustness algorithm is
(
Beillahi
et al. 2021b
)
, which showed that
verifying robustness against causal consistency reduces to the reachability problem under SC. As a means to analyse the robustness problem for
{\tt CC}
,
(
Cerone
et al. 2017
)
provide an algebraic connection between the operational and axiomatic semantics of
{\tt CC}
. In the non-transactional setting,
(
Lahav 2019
)

surveys WRA (analogue of
{\tt CC}
), SRA (analogue of
{\tt CCv}
) and discusses the operational and axiomatic semantics of both models.
(
Raad
et al. 2019
)
proposes a lock-based
reference implementation for
snapshot isolation
(SI), a weak consistency model used in databases such as Oracle and MS SQL server, while prior work
(
Raad
et al. 2018
)
did the same for
parallel snapshot isolation
(PSI), a model closely related to snapshot isolation.
(
Xiong
et al. 2020
)
identify a consistency model
weak snapshot isolation
(WSI) that sits in between SI and PSI and propose an operational semantics for client observable behaviours of atomic transactions.

(
Chong
et al. 2018
)
considers the
interplay between weak memory and transactional memory
(
Herlihy and Moss 1993
)
by extending existing axiomatic weak memory models (x86, Power, ARMv8, and C++) with new rules for transactional memory. They also synthesize tests for validating these models on existing implementations.

In contrast to our paper,
none of the earlier works provide algorithms that can
systematically explore all executions of a concurrent program
under causal consistency and certify them safe against concurrency bugs.

Fig. 1.
Differences between consistency models.
The
\triangleright v
denotes the expected
return value of the read event.

2.
Overview

In this section, we give an overview of some
well-known variants of causal consistency
as well as the working of our DPOR algorithm. Later, we give the denotational semantics needed for our DPOR algorithm (section
4
),
two efficient operations to explore traces (section
5
),
the DPOR algorithm (section
6
) and our experimental results
(section
8
).

Two key notions needed across the models are
that of
causal delivery
and

transaction isolation
.
We discuss these before going into
specific models.
A process is a sequence of transactions, and a transaction is a sequence
of read/write instructions. A transaction is executed atomically,
and no interleaving happens within a transaction.
A
transaction log
is a sequence
(x_{1},v_{1})\dots,(x_{n},v_{n})

obtained from the writes
x_{i}:=v_{i}
made in order in the transaction.
Here,
x_{i}
is a shared variable and
v_{i}
is a value.
The log is reset (to
\epsilon
) before a transaction begins, and every write
x:=v
in the transaction appends
(x,v)
to the log.
Thus, the log of transaction
t_{1}
in the program Figure
1
(a) is
(x,2)(z,2)
. When a transaction finishes, its log is available to be delivered to all processes. This is done respecting causal delivery : if the log of a transaction
t
is received by a process before it starts executing a transaction
t^{\prime}
, then the log of
t
is received by all processes before receiving the log of
t^{\prime}
. Also, if
t,t^{\prime}
are two transactions in program order in a process
p
, with
t^{\prime}
coming later than
t
, then the log of
t
is always (self)delivered to
p
before the execution of
t^{\prime}
. Hence, the log of
t
will be delivered to any process before delivering the log of
t^{\prime}
. Transaction isolation says that while a process is executing a transaction, it will not receive any delivery. Thus, deliveries are received only when a process has finished executing a transaction and not started executing the next transaction. An execution under any of the models respect causal delivery and transaction isolation. Each process has a
local store
, keeping track of the latest values delivered to it, per variable. In a program having shared variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, the local store of all processes is
\{(x_{1},0),\dots,(x_{n},0)\}
initially. 0 is the initial value of all variables.

When a process
p
executes a read
r=x
in its transaction
t
, it first checks
t
made a write to
x
. If yes, it reads the latest value written to
x
in
t
. Otherwise, it takes the value of
x
from its local store.

Overview of
{\tt CM}
. Under
{\tt CM}
, whenever a process receives a delivery, it updates its local store using the latest writes per variable from the log received. For instance, if the
log received is
(x,1)(z,2)(x,2)
, and the local store is
\{(x,3),(y,0),(z,1)\}
, then the store is updated to
\{(x,2),(y,0),(z,2)\}
.

The program in Figure
1
(d) has a
{\tt CM}
consistent execution :
First
p_{b}
executes
t_{2},t_{3}
delivering
t_{2},t_{3}
to itself in order. Then
p_{a}
executes
t_{1}
, delivers the log to
p_{b}
. The local store of
p_{b}
then is
\{(x,2),(y,1),(z,2)\}
. Then
t_{4}
is executed, and
p_{b}
reads off
{\tt{c}}
as 1 and
{\tt{d}}
as 2 from its local store. This is followed by delivering to
p_{c}
,
t_{1},t_{2}
in order before executing
t_{5}
so that the local store of
p_{c}
is
\{(x,2),(y,0),(z,1)\}
. Then
p_{c}
can read 2 into
{\tt{a}}
and 1 into
{\tt{b}}
.
t_{4}
can be delivered to
p_{c}
at the end.

Overview of
{\tt CCv}
. In
{\tt CCv}
, transactions are totally ordered by unique ids (
\in\mathbb{N}
) associated to
transactions. Transactions in program order of a process
have monotonically increasing ids. Each process
p
maintains per variable
x
, the timestamp of
x
,
\mathsf{ts}(x)
.

\mathsf{ts}(x)
is the largest transaction id among all transaction logs delivered to
p
, which has a write to
x
. On receiving the delivery of a log from a transaction
t
, the value of
x
in the local store of process
p
is updated
with the latest value of
x
from the log
only if
\mathsf{ts}(x)
in
p
is smaller than
t.id
. In this case,
\mathsf{ts}(x)
is also updated to
t.id
.

Consider the program in Figure
1
(a). This is not consistent wrt
{\tt CCv}
. We know that
t_{2}.id<t_{3}.id
. To read 2 into
{\tt{b}}
, we need
t_{1}.id>t_{3}.id>t_{2}.id
. However, to read 1 into
{\tt{c}}
, we need
t_{2}.id>t_{1}.id
.

Overview of
{\tt CC}
. In
{\tt CC}
, transaction ids are vector clocks whose dimension equals the number of processes. Transactions in program order of a process have increasing ids.
The local store
of each process is a set of triples of the form
(x,v,t.id)
, where

t.id
is the transaction id of the transaction which wrote
v
to
x
.
For each process
p
, on receiving delivery of the transaction log of transaction
t
, (1)
(x,v,t.id)
is added to the local store of
p
if
t.id
is incomparable with
all the transaction ids of existing triples
in the store of
p
, (2)
(x,v,t.id)
replaces
all
(x,v^{\prime},t^{\prime}.id)
triples in the store of
p
if
t.id>t^{\prime}.id
,
and (3)
(x,v)
is the latest write to
x
in the log of
t
.
Each time a new transaction begins execution in a process
p
, it maintains a
snapshot
of its local store per variable. It assigns to each variable
x
, the latest value
snapshot(x)
obtained from a linearization of the store wrt
x
.
A read instruction
r=x
in a transaction
t
first checks
if
t
has written to
x
, and reads the latest such write if any.
If
t
has not written to
x
, it reads
snapshot(x)
.

The program in Figure
1
(a) is
{\tt CC}
consistent.
Choose
t_{1}.id
incomparable with
t_{2}.id,t_{3}.id
. Let
t_{1}.id=(0,5)
,

t_{2}.id=(1,2),t_{3}.id=(1,3)
.
p_{b}
’s local store after
t_{2},t_{3}
is
\{(z,1,t_{2}.id),(y,1,t_{3},id),(x,1,t_{3}.id)\}
. On delivering the log of
t_{1}
to
p_{b}
,
(x,2,t_{1}.id),(z,2,t_{1}.id)
are added to the local store of
p_{b}
.
When
p_{b}
starts
t_{4}
, it maintains

snapshot(x)=2
(linearizing
\{(x,2,t_{1}.id),(x,1,t_{3}.id)\}

as
t_{3}t_{1}
),
snapshot(z)=1
(linearizing
\{(z,2,t_{1}.id),(z,1,t_{2}.id)\}
as
t_{1}t_{2}
), and
snapshot(y)=1
.
This enables the reads in
t_{4}
.

Overview of
{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
. Like
{\tt CCv}
, in
{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
also, transactions are totally ordered by unique ids (
\in\mathbb{N}
).
In
{\tt RCom}
, for transactions
t_{1},t_{2}
writing to some variable
x
, if the latest writes on
x
by
t_{1},t_{2}

are read by events
r_{1},r_{2}
appearing in a transaction
t
such that
r_{2}
appears later than
r_{1}
in
t
,
then
t_{1}.id<t_{2}.id
. Any
{\tt RCom}
consistent execution orders transactions monotonically wrt respective
monotonicity of reads.
The program Fig.
1
(a) is not
{\tt RCom}
consistent. Consider the read events
e_{1},e_{2}
as
{\tt{a}}:=y
and
{\tt{b}}:=x
in
t_{4}
reading respectively from
t_{3},t_{1}
. Then we have
t_{3}.id<t_{1}.id
. Likewise,

t_{4}
has the read event
e_{3}
given by
{\tt{c}}:=z
reading from
t_{2}
.
Then
t_{1}.id<t_{2}.id
which is not possible.
{\tt RAt}
is a strengthening of
{\tt RCom}
wrt the ordering of transactions.
For all variables
x
and all
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}

if a transaction
t_{3}
in some process
p
reads from the latest write of
t_{1}
on
x
, then any transaction
t_{2}
which is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-before
t_{3}
in
p
, or which reads from
t_{3}

must be such that

t_{2}.id<t_{1}.id
. For the program Fig.
1
(a), we have

t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
and
t_{4}
reads from
t_{1}
. Since
t_{3}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-before
t_{4}
, we have
t_{3}.id<t_{1}.id
.
t_{4}
reads from
t_{2}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
and since

t_{4}
reads from
t_{1}
(for reading into
{\tt{b}}
), we have
t_{1}.id<t_{2}.id
. This gives
t_{3}.id<<t_{1}.id<t_{2}.id
which is not possible.

Fig. 2.
The DPOR run tree on program Fig
1
(a) wrt
{\tt CC},{\tt CM},{\tt CCv}

Overview of DPOR
.
We give a quick overview of the key elements in
our DPOR algorithm.
Consider the program in Figure
1
(a)
and the assertion
\varphi:{\tt{a}}=1\wedge{\tt{b}}=2\wedge{\tt{c}}=1
.
The DPOR algorithm non-deterministically chooses to execute
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
since
the program can start with
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
. Assume that the DPOR algorithm non-deterministically
executes
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}

in order, and finally comes to
t_{4}
. This choice does not affect the final outcome. The algorithm will not have a redundant exploration where it begins with
t_{2}
.
The reason is that once we fix a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
for the reads in
t_{4}
, all the three executions
t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow t_{3}\rightarrow t_{4}
,

t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{3}\rightarrow t_{4}
,

t_{2}\rightarrow t_{3}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{4}

result in the same
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
trace and
are equivalent. The optimality of our algorithm
ensures that we do not explore any trace more than once. Let us first consider the
{\tt CCv}
DPOR.

[\bf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1)}}]
The first read event of
t_{4}

has only one source
to read from, namely the write
y:=1
in
t_{3}
.

[\bf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(2)}}]
The next read event of
t_{4}
is on
{\tt{b}}
;
it has two possible sources to read from :
t_{1}
and

t_{3}
. The DPOR detects the

readable set
of transactions at this point as

\{t_{1},t_{3}\}
, and proceeds with
two branches corresponding to these choices.

[\bf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(3)}}]
Consider
the branch where
{\tt{b}}
reads from the
write
x:=1
of
t_{3}
.
It then proceeds with the next read in
t_{4}
on
{\tt{c}}
. It creates two branches corresponding to the two sources to read from, namely,
t_{1},t_{2}
.
If it reads from
t_{2}
, we obtain trace
\tau_{2}
.

[\bf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(4)}}]

Since it did not witness
\varphi
, the algorithm backtracks to the first point where it had the alternate
source to read for
{\tt{c}}
, namely,
t_{1}
. Here, it detects
two causal dependencies created by reading
{\tt{c}}
from
t_{1}
.
First it detects a causality between
t_{2},t_{1}
since
t_{2}
also writes on
z
. To resolve this, it orders
t_{2}
before
t_{1}
. Second,
it also detects a causality between
t_{1}
and
t_{3}

since both write to
x
, and both can reach
t_{4}
.
Since it reads
x
from
t_{3}
, it orders
t_{1}

before
t_{3}
.
The obtained trace is
\tau_{1}
. The purple edges (see Figure
2
) between
t_{3},t_{2}
and
t_{1}
represent the orderings
made by the DPOR to resolve the causal dependencies
after the read of
{\tt{c}}
. The blue edges labeled with variables
x,y,z
represents reading from the writes of
x,y,z
. The red edges represent the program order.

[\bf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(5)}}]

Once again, since it did not witness
\varphi
, it backtracks to the first point where it had the alternate
source to read for
{\tt{b}}
, namely from the write

x:=2
in
t_{1}
. Here it detects a causality between
t_{3}
and
t_{1}

since both write on
x
, and both can reach
t_{4}
. To resolve this, it orders
t_{3}
before
t_{1}
. It then proceeds with the next read in
t_{4}
on
{\tt{c}}
. Here, it detects that
{\tt{c}}
cannot read from
t_{2}
since
t_{3}
is ordered before
t_{1}
.
It therefore reads from the write
z:=2
of
t_{1}
. After the read, it orders
t_{2}
before
t_{1}
.
The obtained trace
is
\tau_{3}
.
At the end of this branch, it returns having finished all possible exlporations and concludes that

\varphi
cannot be witnessed
in any
{\tt CCv}
consistent execution.

However, if we run the algorithm under
{\tt CC}
,
then we get an extra trace
\tau_{4}
. This is obtained since the algorithm
allows in step
(\bf{5})
above, to read
{\tt{c}}

from
t_{2}
.

Under
{\tt CC}
, the algorithm detects that even though
it ordered
t_{3}
before
t_{1}
wrt
x
(as in step
(\bf{5})
above), it can now
order
t_{1}
before
t_{2}
since it is dealing with a read on a variable
z\neq x
. This allows it to witness the assertion

\varphi
. Finally, if we consider
{\tt CM}
, once again, we do not witness

\varphi
. The algorithm in this case produces three traces
which are similar to those in
{\tt CCv}
.

3.
Preliminaries

Programs
.
We consider transactional programs
{\mathcal{P}}
consisting of a finite set of
threads
or
processes

p\in{\mathsf{Procs}}
that share a finite set
{\mathbb{X}}
of shared variables
ranging over
a domain
{\mathbb{V}}
of
values
that includes a special
value
0
.
A process has a finite set
of local registers that store values from
{\mathbb{V}}
.
Each process runs a deterministic code, built in a standard way
from expressions and atomic commands, using standard control
flow constructs (sequential composition, selection, and bounded loop constructs). Throughout the paper, we use
x,y
for shared variables,

{\tt a},{\tt b},{\tt c}
for registers, and
e
for expressions.
Expressions do not contain any shared variables.

Each process is an ordered sequence of
transactions
.
A transaction is an ordered sequence of
labeled instructions
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
denote the set of all transactions in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Transactions are denoted by
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},\dots
.
A transaction
t\in\mathcal{T}
in process
p
starts with a
\mathtt{begin}
(p,t)
instruction and ends with an
\mathtt{end}
(p,t)
instruction (in the figures, we simply enclose the code of a transaction in a box without explicit
\mathtt{begin}
and
\mathtt{end}
statements).
The instructions in a transaction are write/read/assign instructions apart from standard conditional flow constructs.
Write instructions
x:=e
write to the shared variable
x
while read instructions
r:=x
read from the shared variables
x
. Assignments have the form
r:=e
involving local registers and expressions.
We assume that the control cannot pass from one transaction to another in a process without going through the
\mathtt{begin}
and
\mathtt{end}
instructions.

Each transaction has a transaction identifier
t.id
(which is
in
\mathbb{N}
or
\mathbb{N}^{|{\mathsf{Procs}}|}
depending on the model
X\in\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}\}
we consider).
If process
p
is the ordered sequence
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{n}
of transactions, then,
t_{i}.id<t_{j}.id
for
i<j
.

The local state of a process
{\it proc}\in{\mathsf{Procs}}

is defined by its program counter (which also makes it clear which transaction
t
the control resides in each process), the contents
of its registers and shared variables.
A
configuration
of
{\mathcal{P}}

is made up of the local states of all the processes.
A program execution is a sequence of transitions between configurations, starting with the initial configuration
\gamma^{\mathsf{init}}
.
Each transition corresponds to one process performing an instruction in one of its transactions.
A transition between two configurations
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
is of form

\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\leadsto}}\gamma^{\prime}
,
where
\ell\in\{\mathsf{begin}(p,t),\mathsf{end}(p,t),x:=v,{\tt{a}}:=x\}
.
Since assignments involve only registers, they are not visible to other
processes, we will not represent them explicitly
in the transition relation. Instead,
we let each transition represent the combined effect of some
finite sequence of assignments in a transaction followed
by a read/write/
\mathtt{end}
statement in the same transaction.

The labels represent starting and ending of transaction
t
in process
p
, executing a read/write instruction in a transaction. Between labels

\mathsf{begin}(p,t),\mathsf{end}(p,t)
, we only execute instructions of transaction
t
. We follow a more succinct representation
for a run by replacing the contiguous sequence of transitions
\sigma_{i}\xrightarrow{{\tt{begin}}(p,t)}\sigma_{i+1}\dots\xrightarrow{{\tt{end}}(p,t)}\sigma_{j}
corresponding to the execution of a transaction with
\sigma_{i}\xrightarrow{{\tt{issue}}(p,t)}\sigma_{j}
.

\mathtt{issue}
(p,t)
represents
issuing
the transaction
t
in process
p
, and signifies executing all instructions of
t
in order,
starting with
\mathtt{begin}
(p,t)
and ending with
\mathtt{end}
(p,t)
.

The values which can be read on a read instruction appearing in a transaction depend on the consistency model under consideration. This is taken care of by associating runs with so-called
traces
which we define in section
4
. Traces
tell us the values reads can obtain from available
writes. A causal consistency model
X\in\{{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom},{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM}\}

is formulated by imposing
restrictions on traces, thereby also restricting the possible
runs that are associated with them.

A configuration
\gamma
is said to be
terminal
if

{\tt succ}\!\left(\gamma\right)=\emptyset
, i.e., no process can issue any transaction from

\gamma
.
A
run

\rho
from
\gamma
is a sequence

\gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}}{}\gamma_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell_{2}}{}\cdots\xrightarrow{\ell_{n}}{}\gamma_{n}

such that
\gamma_{0}=\gamma
.
We say that
\rho
is
terminated
if
\gamma_{n}
is terminal.
We let
{\it Runs}(\gamma)
denote the set of runs
from
\gamma
.

Events
.
An event corresponds to a particular execution
of a statement in a run of
{\mathcal{P}}
.
A
write event

\mathit{ev}
in a transaction
t
is given by
(id,{\it proc},t,\mathsf{wt}(x,v))
where

id\in{\mathbb{N}}
is the identifier of the event,
{\it proc}
is the process containing the event in transaction
t
,

x\in{\mathbb{X}}
is a variable, and

v\in{\mathbb{V}}
is a value.
This
corresponds to a write event
happening in transaction
t
of process
{\it proc}

writing the value
v
to variable
x
.
Likewise, a
read event

\mathit{ev}
is given by

(id,{\it proc},t,\mathsf{rd}(x))
where
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
.
The read event
\mathit{ev}
does not specify the particular
value it reads;
this value will be defined in a trace by specifying a write event
from which
\mathit{ev}
fetches its value.

For each variable
x\in{\mathbb{X}}
, we assume
a special transaction containing only (apart from
\mathtt{begin,end}
) the write event
\mathsf{init}_{x}=\mathsf{wt}(x,0)

called the
initializer
transaction
t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}
for
x
.
This transaction is not part of any of the processes in
{\mathsf{Procs}}
, and
writes the value
0
to
x
.
We define
\mathcal{T}_{\it init}:=\left\{{t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}}\mid{x\in{\mathbb{X}}}\right\}
as
the set of initializer transactions.
Let
\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x},\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
respectively denote the set of transactions having a write and read instruction on
x
.
If
{\tt E}
is a set of events, we define
subsets of
{\tt E}
characterized
by particular attributes of its events.
For instance, for a variable
x
, we let
{\tt E}^{{\mathsf{rd}},x,t}

denote
\left\{{\mathit{ev}\in{\tt E}}\mid{{\mathit{ev}}.{\it type}={\mathsf{rd}}\land{\mathit{ev}}.{\it var}=x\land{\mathit{ev}}.{\it tran}=t}\right\}
, for all read events in transaction
t
on variable
x
.

Traces
.
A
trace

\tau
is a tuple
\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
,
where
\mathcal{T}
is the set of all
transactions

including the set
\mathcal{T}_{\it init}
of initializer transactions,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
(program order),

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
(read-from) are binary relations on
\mathcal{T}
that satisfy:

\bullet

t~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t^{\prime}
if
\mathit{process}(t)=\mathit{process}(t^{\prime})
and
t.id<t^{\prime}.id
. The relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}

totally orders the transactions of each
individual process. We assume that

t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t
for all transactions
t\in\mathcal{T}
for all variables
x
.

\bullet

t~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t^{\prime}
if
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
,
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
, and the value read is the value written by the last write
w
in
t
.

We can view
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
as a graph whose nodes
are the transactions
\mathcal{T}
and whose edges are defined by the
relations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
.
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
is depicted by red solid edges and captures the order of transactions in each process while
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
edges are depicted as solid blue edges.

We define the
empty trace

\tau_{\emptyset}:=\left\langle\mathcal{T}_{\it init},\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle
,
containing only the initializer transactions,
and all relations empty.

We define when a trace can be associated with a run.
Consider a run
\rho

\gamma_{0}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}}{}\cdots\xrightarrow{\ell_{n}}{}\gamma_{n}
,

and let
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
be a trace.
We write
\rho\models\tau
to denote that
the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)
\mathcal{T}=\{{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}}\}
,
i.e.,
each transaction
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
corresponds exactly to one label
\mathsf{issue}(p,t_{i})
in
\rho
.

(ii) For
\ell_{i}=\mathsf{issue}(p,t_{i}),\ell_{j}=\mathsf{issue}(p,t_{j})
,

i<j
iff
t_{i}.id<t_{j}.id
.

(iii)

t_{i}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\;t_{j}
iff
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x},t_{j}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}

for some variable
x
, and
\ell_{k}=\mathsf{issue}(p_{i},t_{i})
,

\ell_{h}=\mathsf{issue}(p_{j},t_{j})
for
k<h
, and the values written and read in
t_{i},t_{j}
are the same.

(iv)
if
t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{i}
, then
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}

and
the value read is 0.

4.
Causally Consistent Models

Our DPOR algorithm relies on the
declarative
definition
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017b
)
,
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
of causal consistency models. Under the declarative semantics, the program is associated with a set of
traces
.
Each trace summarizes a particular program execution and describes all accesses to the shared variables and the relations between them in that run. To define the five models
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
,
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017b
)
formally, we introduce a function that, for each model, extends a given trace uniquely by a set of new edges. Then we define the model by requiring that the extended trace does not contain any cycles. A run of the program satisfies a consistency model

X\in\{{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom},{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM}\}

if its associated extended trace has no cycles.

Let
\mathsf{CO}
, called
causality order
represent
({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}})^{+}
. Two transactions
t_{1},t_{2}
are
causally related
if either
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}
or
t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{1}
.

Fig. 3.
solid red, blue edges are
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
.

Weak Causal Consistency
{\tt CC}
.
We start presenting one of the weakest notions of causal consistency,
{\tt CC}

(
Hamza 2015
)
,
(
Ahamad et al. 1995
)
.
In
{\tt CC}
, transactions writing to a variable which are not causally related can be ordered differently in different transactions in a process. If
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
are causally unrelated, for
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
,
t
can read from the latest write of
t_{1}
or
t_{2}
(choose one); likewise
t^{\prime}
can independently read from one of
t_{1},t_{2}
.

To illustrate, consider the program Fig.
1
(a).
The transaction
t_{1}
is not causally related to transactions
t_{2},t_{3}
.
Hence, the read from
x
in
t_{4}
can read from either of
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
; likewise, the read from
z
in
t_{4}
can also choose to read from either of
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
.

Finally, if in
p_{b}
has transaction
t_{5}
(after
t_{4}
) having read
{\tt{d}}:=x
,
{\tt{d}}
can read 1 by choosing to read from
t_{3}
(and not
t_{1}
).

A trace
\tau
does not violate
{\tt CC}
as long as there is a causality order which explains the return value of each read event.
To capture traces violating
{\tt CC}
, we define a relation
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
between transactions (
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
represents overwrite) which write to the same variable. For transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}

and
t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
, if
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{3}
, and
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
, then

t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}~t_{1}
. This says that
t_{3}
reads an earlier write by
t_{1}
, resulting in a
\mathsf{CO}~\cup~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
cycle. We refer to
\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
cycles as
\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

We define a function

\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CC}}(\tau)
which extends a trace

\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
by adding all possible
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
edges
between transactions which write on the same variable.

For a trace
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
,

\tau\models{\tt CC}
iff
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CC}}(\tau)

does not have

\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

Fig. 4.
Start with (a). In (b) we add the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}}
edge from
t_{6}
to
t_{2}
following condition (ii).
(c) is obtained on adding
t_{8}
and
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{8}
. In contrast, (b)’ does not follow condition (ii). Hence, when
t_{8}
is added in (c)’,

t_{5}
is available to be read (since we do not have the path from
t_{5}
to
t_{3}
, making
t_{5}
an overwritten write on
x
).
Choosing
t_{5}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{8}
necessitates adding
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}}~t_{5}
in (d)’ by condition (i).
This necessitates adding

t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}}~t_{1}
in (e)’ creating
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

Examples
.
Program Fig.
1
(b) is not
{\tt CC}
since there is no causality
order which explains the return values of the read events. If we consider any trace (Fig.
3
)
of the program Fig.
1
(b), we find that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
(for reading 2 into
{\tt{c}}
),
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
(for reading 1 into

{\tt{a}}
), and
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
(for reading 1 into
{\tt{b}}
).
This induces
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{4}
(
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
).
Since
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
,
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{4}
with

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
creates
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}~t_{1}
witnessing
\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

Causal Convergence
{\tt CCv}
.
Under
{\tt CCv}
, we need a total order on all transactions which write to the same variable. This order, called
arbitration order
, is an abstraction of how conflicts are resolved by all processes to agree upon one ordering among transactions which are not causally related.
Thus, unlike
{\tt CC}
,
different transactions in a process cannot independently choose to read from different causally unrelated transactions.
To enforce a total order between transactions writing to the same variable, we use a new relation
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
called conflict relation on transactions writing to the same variable.
For all variables
x\in\mathcal{V}
, transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
and
t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
,
if
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{3}
, and
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
then
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
.

We define a function

\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CCv}}(\tau)
which extends a trace

\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
by adding all possible
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges
between transactions writing on the same variable.
Traces violating
{\tt CCv}
exhibit a

\mathsf{CO}~\cup~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~\cup~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}
cycle in
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CCv}}(\tau)
, which we refer to as
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.
We say that
\tau\models{\tt CCv}
iff
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CCv}}(\tau)
does not contain a
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

Examples
. For the program Fig.
1
(a) and any trace
\tau
,

\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CCv}}(\tau)

has a
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
(see Fig.
3
)
since in any trace, we have
t_{3}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{4}
,
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
(this
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
is unavoidable to read 2 into
{\tt{b}}
) with

t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
giving
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
.
Now we have
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{4}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
and
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
(this
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
is needed to read 1 into
{\tt{c}}
).
This gives
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
resulting in
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
.
Intuitively, we cannot find a total order amongst the transactions

t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
. Likewise, Fig.
1
(d) exhibits
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

Causal Memory
{\tt CM}
.
The
{\tt CM}
model is stronger than
{\tt CC}
and incomparable to
{\tt CCv}
.
In
{\tt CM}
, a process can diverge from another one in its ordering of transactions which are not causally related. However, once a process chooses an ordering of such transactions, all reading transactions in it adhere to it; this makes it stronger than
{\tt CC}
and incomparable to
{\tt CCv}
.

A
happened before
relation per process fixes the per process ordering of transactions.
For a transaction
t
in a trace, the
Causal Past
of
t
,
\mathsf{CausalPast}(t)=\{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}\mid t^{\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t\}
is the set of transactions
which are in the causal past of
t
.

For a transaction
t
, the happened before relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}

is the smallest relation on transactions which is transitive, and is such that for all transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in

\mathsf{CausalPast}(t)
,
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}\Rightarrow t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}~t_{2}
. In other words,
\mathsf{CO}_{\mid\mathsf{CausalPast}(t)}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}
:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}
contains all pairs of transactions obtained by restricting
\mathsf{CO}
to the transactions in the causal past of
t
. For any variable
x
, if we have transactions

t_{3},t_{4}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
,
t_{5}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}

such that
(i)
t_{5}{=}t
or
t_{5}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t
,
t_{4}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{5}
, and
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}~t_{5}
, then
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}~t_{4}
, and
(ii) if
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}~t_{4}
,
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{5}
, then

t_{5}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}~t_{4}
.

Let
t_{p}
be the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-last transaction of process
p
: that is, for all transactions
t
in process
p
,
t=t_{p}
or
t~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{p}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t_{p}}}
for all transactions
t
in process
p
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t_{p}}}
fixes the ordering among all causally unrelated transactions for process
p
. We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{t_{p}}}
.

We define a function
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CM}}
which extends a trace
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
by adding all possible
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}
edges for all processes
p
. Traces violating
{\tt CM}
exhibit
a
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}
cycle, called a
\mathsf{CMcycle}

in
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CM}}(\tau)
for some process
p
.
We say that
\tau\models{\tt CM}
iff
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CM}}(\tau)
does not contain a
\mathsf{CMcycle}
. Figure
4
motivates conditions (i), (ii) to add
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}}
edges so that

\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CM}}(\tau)
does not contain
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

Examples
.
For the program Fig.
1
(a) and any trace
\tau
,
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt CM}}(\tau)
contains
\mathsf{CMcycle}
. Consider
transaction
t_{4}
in
p_{b}
. We have
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
, and

t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{4}
with
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
, obtaining

t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{1}
. Now, we have
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{4}
, that is,

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{4}
and
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
, with
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}

obtaining
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{2}
. This gives
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{3}
resulting in
\mathsf{CMcycle}
. Likewise, in the program Fig.
1
(b), we have
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{c}}}~t_{4}

and
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
resulting in

t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{c}}}~t_{1}
and therefore the
\mathsf{CMcycle}

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{c}}}~t_{1}
.
For the program in Fig.
1
(c), we have

t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
inducing
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{4}
. Now, we have
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}

and
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
, obtaining
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{3}
.
We also have
t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
. Along with

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{3}
and
t_{1},t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
gives

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}
. Then we have the
t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p_{b}}}~t_{\mathsf{init}_{x}}
obtaining
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

Read Committed
{\tt RCom}
.

{\tt RCom}
is one of the weakest models for causal consistency
(
Berenson et al. 1995b
)
. Like
{\tt CCv}
, the consistency condition is defined by extending traces with the
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
relation between transactions writing to the same variable.
For transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
for some variable
x
,

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
is defined by ensuring that the
corresponding read events appearing in a transaction
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
are
monotonic
. To capture
monotonicity between events in a transaction, we use the
transaction order

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}
which orders events within a transaction. If
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{k}
is the ordered sequence of events in a transaction
t
, define
e_{i}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~e_{j}
for
i<j
.
The intuition is that for
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\tt E}^{{\mathsf{rd}},t}
with
e_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~e_{2}
, if
e_{1}\in{\tt E}^{{\mathsf{rd}},x,t}
reads from a transaction
t_{1}
, and
e_{2}\in{\tt E}^{{\mathsf{rd}},y,t}
reads from a transaction
t_{2}
, and
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
, then

t_{2}
must be ”
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
-later” than
t_{1}
, that is,
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
.
That is, for all variables
x
, and all transactions
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
, if there exist read events
e_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~e_{2}
in
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~e_{1},t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~e_{2}
, then
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
.

Define a function
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RCom}}(\tau)
which extends a trace
\tau=(\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}})
by
adding all possible
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges between transactions which write on the same variable.
In
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RCom}}(\tau)
, we do not draw the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
edges
from transactions
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
to read events

e\in{\tt E}^{{\mathsf{rd}},x,t^{\prime}}
; these are used to draw the
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges between transactions.
Traces violating
{\tt RCom}
exhibit a
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
cycle in
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RCom}}(\tau)
, which we refer to as a
\mathsf{RComcycle}
. We say that
\tau\models{\tt RCom}
iff
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RCom}}(\tau)
does not contain a
\mathsf{RComcycle}
.

Examples
. For the program Fig.
1
(a), and any trace
\tau
,
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RCom}}(\tau)
contains
\mathsf{RComcycle}
.
Consider the read events
e_{1}={\tt{a}}:=y
,
e_{2}={\tt{b}}:=x
in
t_{4}
,
such that
e_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~e_{2}
and
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~e_{1}
,
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~e_{2}
, with
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
. Then we have
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
. Likewise,

t_{4}
has the read event
e_{3}={\tt{c}}:=z
with
e_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~e_{3}
.
Since
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~e_{3}
, we obtain
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
resulting in a
cycle. Likewise, for the program Fig.
1
(d), we obtain
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
(reads in
t_{5}
) as well as
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
(reads in
t_{4}
).

Read Atomic
{\tt RAt}
. This model
(
Cerone
et al. 2015
)
is a strengthening
of
{\tt RCom}
in how we add
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges between transactions writing
to the same variable. While
{\tt RCom}
enforced a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
-ordering
between transactions
t_{1},t_{2}
writing to the same variable
based on the
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}
ordering of the corresponding read events, this
is strengthened in
{\tt RAt}
as follows.
For a transaction
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
which is read by a read event in a transaction
t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
, any transaction
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
for which

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{2}
or
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{2}
must be such that
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
.

For all variables
x
and all
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
,
t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
, if
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{3}
or
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{3}
, then we have
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
.
Define a function
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RAt}}(\tau)
which extends a trace
\tau=(\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}})
by
adding all possible
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges between transactions which write on the same variable.
Traces violating
{\tt RAt}
exhibit a
\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
cycle in

\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RAt}}(\tau)
, which we refer to as a
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
. We say that
\tau\models{\tt RAt}
iff
\mathsf{extend}_{{\tt RAt}}(\tau)
does not contain a
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
.

Examples
. For the program Fig.
1
(a), we have
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
with
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
and
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{4}
giving
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
. Likewise, we also have
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
with
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
and

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}{\tt rf}}~t_{4}
(for reading into
{\tt{b}}
) giving
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}

resulting in
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
exhibiting a
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
. Likewise, for the program in Fig.
1
(d),
we have
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
(since
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{4}
,
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t_{4}
and
t_{1},t_{3}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
)
and also
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{2}
(since
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{5}
,
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{5}
and

t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},z}
) giving a
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
.

A run
\rho
satisfies a model
X\in\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}\}
if there exists a trace
\tau
such that
\rho\models\tau
and
\tau\models X
.
Define

\gamma_{X}:=\{\tau_{X}\mid\exists\rho\in{\it Runs}(\gamma).\rho\models\tau_{X}\land\tau_{X}\models X\}
,
the set of traces generated
under
X
from a given configuration
\gamma
.

5.
Trace Semantics

To analyse a program
{\mathcal{P}}
under a model
X\in\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom}\}
,
all runs of
{\mathcal{P}}
must be explored. We do this by exploring the associated traces. In fact, two runs having the same associated traces are equivalent
since the assertions to be checked at the end of a run depend only on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
. We begin with the empty trace, and continue exploration
by adding enabled read/write events to the traces generated so far. While doing this, we must ensure that the generated traces
\tau
are s.t.
\tau\models X
.
We present two efficient operations to add a new transaction to
a trace
\tau
obtaining a trace
\tau^{\prime}
so that
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{\prime})
does not contain a
\mathsf{Xcycle}
.

Readability and Visibility
. For all 5 models, readability identifies the transactions
t^{\prime}

from which read events of a newly added transaction
t
can read/fetch their value. Visibility is used to add, in the case of
{\tt CCv}
,
{\tt RAt}
and
{\tt RCom}

new
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edges (and in the case of
{\tt CM}
, new
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}}
edges) that are implied by the fact that the read event in the new transaction
t
reads from
t^{\prime}
. Let
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle
be a trace, and
\tau_{X}=\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau)
.
Let
\tau^{t}_{X}
denote adding
t
to
\tau_{X}
.

We define the readable set
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
for a transaction
t
containing a read event on variable
x
as the set of transactions

t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
from which
t
can read from. Intuitively,
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
contains all
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
which are not hidden in
\tau^{t}_{X}
by other
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
. The newly added transaction
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},x}
can fetch its value from a transaction in
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
.

(1)

For
X={\tt RCom}
, we define
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)

as the set of all transactions
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
provided we do not have a transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that the following is true.
Assume
\beta~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~\alpha
are two read events in
t
such that
\beta
reads from
t^{\prime\prime}
,
\alpha
(current read event) reads from
t^{\prime}
and

t^{\prime}~(\mathsf{CO}~\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t^{\prime\prime}
. Note that having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
induces

t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
and a
\mathsf{RComcycle}
.

(2)

For
X={\tt CC}
,
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is defined as the set of all transactions
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
s.t.

•

there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
s.t.
t^{\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t^{\prime\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t
in
\tau^{t}_{X}
.
Allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
) in the presence of such a
t^{\prime\prime}
gives
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}~t^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

•

In case
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(
t^{\prime}
also writes on some
y\neq x
) and
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},y}
,
there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
) and
t^{\prime\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t^{\prime}
. Note that having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
, and
allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
)
results in
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}~t^{\prime\prime}
(wrt
y
) and
\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

•

if
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},y}
(for some
y\neq x
) there are no transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
),
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t^{\prime}
. Assuming we have
this, then allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
) gives
t_{1}~\mathsf{CO}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t

and hence
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}\mathsf{OW}}~t_{1}
(wrt
y
) and
\mathsf{CCcycle}
.

(3)

For
X={\tt CCv}
,
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},r,x)
is defined as the set of all transactions
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
s.t.

•

there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that

t^{\prime}~(\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t^{\prime\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t
. If we have this
t^{\prime\prime}
, allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(
t^{\prime}
also writes on some
y\neq x
), there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
).
Having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
with
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
) results in
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime\prime}
(wrt
x
) and
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
(wrt
y
), and
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(
t^{\prime}
also writes on some
y\neq x
), there is no transaction

t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
) and

t^{\prime\prime}~(\mathsf{CO}~\cup~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}t^{\prime}
. Having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
along with

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
) results in
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime\prime}
(wrt
y
) and
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

•

There are no transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
) such that

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
) and
t_{1}~(\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t
. Having such
t_{1},t_{2}
, along with
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t_{1}~({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}\cup\mathsf{CO})^{+}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t
. With
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
, we get
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
and
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

•

If
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{rd},y},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for some
y\neq x
), there are no transactions
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that

t_{1}~(\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t^{\prime}
. If we have this, allowing

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
, resulting
in
\mathsf{CCvcycle}
.

(4)

Let
t
be a transaction in process
p
. For
X={\tt CM}
,
{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is defined as the set of all transactions
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
s.t.

•

there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t
. Having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
and allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
), there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
).
Having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
with
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
x
) results in
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}
,
t~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
), there is no transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
). Having such a
t^{\prime\prime}
with
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime\prime}
giving
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

•

There are no transactions
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
), and
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}
.
Having such
t_{1},t_{2}
with
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t
. With

t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
we get
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{1}
and
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
), there are no transactions
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
),
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t
. If so, allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives

t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}
. Also,
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t,t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{2}
gives
t~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{2}
.
Now, we have
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{2}
obtaining
\mathsf{CMcycle}
.

(5)

For

there is no transaction

. Having such a

.

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for

) there is no transaction

such that

(wrt

). Allowing

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime\prime}
and

t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
), there is no transaction

such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt

) and

. Having such a

with
