and
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
(for
y\neq x
), there are no transactions
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
),
t_{1}~(\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t_{2}~({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}})~t
.
If so, allowing
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
gives
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
(
t_{2},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
) as well as

t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
(
t_{1},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
). This gives

t_{1}~(\mathsf{CO}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}})^{+}~t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
and
\mathsf{RAtcycle}
.

After adding
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
, we must check that
there are no consistency violations.
The check set
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is defined as the set of transactions which turn “sensitive”
on adding the new edge
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
.
Unless appropriate edges are added involving
these sensitive transactions, we may get consistency violating cycles
in the resultant trace.
Let
\tau^{tt^{\prime}}
denote the trace obtained by adding the new transaction
t
and the edge
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
to trace
\tau
. Now, we
identify the
visible set
, that is,
the “sensitive transaction set”
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
and the new edges which must be added to
\tau^{tt^{\prime}}
to obtain a consistent extended trace.

(1)

For
X={\tt RCom}
,
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)=\{t^{\prime\prime}\mid
there is a read event
\beta
in
t
reading from
t^{\prime\prime}
,

\beta~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}to}~\alpha
, and
\alpha
reads from
t^{\prime}\}
. The newly added
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.5,0.06}{\tt rf}}~t
is due to this
\alpha
.
This necessitates
adding
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime}
to preserve consistency. Then
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
contains
\{(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)\}
.

(2)

For
X={\tt RAt}
,
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is classified into two categories.

•

The first kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

\{t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)\mid t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t\}
.
Then we add from each
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
edge to
t^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

•

The second kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are
transactions
t^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
when

t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
. Then we add
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t^{\prime\prime}
to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

(3)

For
X={\tt CCv},{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is classified into three categories.

(a)

The first kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

\{t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)\mid t^{\prime\prime}~\mathsf{CO}~t\}
.
Then we add from each
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}
to
t^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

(b)

The second kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
) when
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
. Then we add
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

(c)

The third kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t_{2}~\mathsf{CO}~t^{\prime}

and we have
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
(wrt
y
) for some
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
. In this case, we add
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{CF}}~t_{1}
to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

(4)

For
X={\tt CM}
,
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
is classified into three categories. Let transaction
t
be in process
p
.

•

The first kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

\{t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)\mid t^{\prime\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t\}
.
Then we add from each
t^{\prime\prime}\in{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}
edge to
t^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

•

The second kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
when
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
. Then we add
t^{\prime}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{1}
to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

•

The third kind of transactions in
{\tt{check}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
are

t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
such that
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t^{\prime}

when we have
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t
for some
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
.
Then we add
t_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{1}
to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.
Adding these
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}
edges, can result in
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{4}
for some
t_{3},t_{4}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},y}
.
If
t_{3}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}~t_{1}
and
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}~t
then we add
t_{1}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.7}\mathsf{HB}_{p}}~t_{4}

to
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
.

The
trace semantics
for a model
X\in\{{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt},{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM}\}
is given as the transition relation
\xrightarrow{}_{X{-}{\tt tr}}
, defined as

\tau_{X}\xrightarrow{\alpha}_{X{-}{\tt tr}}\tau^{\prime}_{X}
where
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau)=\tau_{X},\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{\prime})=\tau^{\prime}_{X}
.
The label
\alpha
is one of
\mathsf{read}(t,t^{\prime}),\mathsf{begin}(t),\mathsf{end}(t),\mathsf{write}(t)

representing respectively, a transaction
t
reading from a transaction
t^{\prime}
, the beginning, end
of a transaction
t
, as well as a write event in
t
.
An important property of
\tau_{X}\xrightarrow{\alpha}_{X{-}{\tt tr}}\tau^{\prime}_{X}
is that if
\tau_{X}
does not have
\mathsf{Xcycle}
, then
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
also does
not have
\mathsf{Xcycle}
; in other words, if
\tau\models X
, then
\tau^{\prime}\models X
.

We now describe the transitions
\tau_{X}\xrightarrow{\alpha}_{X{-}{\tt tr}}\tau^{\prime}_{X}
where
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau)=\tau_{X},\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{\prime})=\tau^{\prime}_{X}
,
\tau=\left\langle\mathcal{T},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}\right\rangle,\tau^{\prime}=\left\langle\mathcal{T}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}^{\prime}\right\rangle
. We start from the empty trace
\tau_{0}
,
\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau_{0})=\tau_{0}
. For
\alpha\in\{\mathsf{end}(t),\mathsf{write}(t)\}
,
\tau^{\prime}_{X}=\tau_{X}
.

•

Assume we observe the start of a transaction
t
in
\tau_{X}
. Then the label
\alpha
is
\mathsf{begin}(t)
, and

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\cup\{t\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}\cup t^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}]t
where
t^{\prime},t
are transactions in the same process
p
,
t^{\prime}
is the last transaction executed in
p
, and

t
is the next transaction in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
order of
p
. This gives
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
.

•

Assume that
t
reads from
t^{\prime}
in
\tau_{X}
. Then the label
\alpha
is
\mathsf{read}(t,t^{\prime})
.
Add the transaction
t
, a
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
edge from the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-latest transaction in process
p
containing
t
in
\tau_{X}
to
t
. Add
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
from a
t^{\prime}\in{\tt{readable}}(\tau^{t}_{X},t,x)
to
t
obtaining
\tau^{tt^{\prime}}
.
Then obtain

\tau^{\prime}_{X}=\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{tt^{\prime}})
as discussed above.

Lemma 5.1.

If
\tau_{X}=\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau)
with
\tau\models X
, and
\tau_{X}\xrightarrow{\alpha}_{{\tt tr}}\tau^{\prime}_{X}=\mathsf{extend}_{X}(\tau^{\prime})
, then
\tau^{\prime}\models X
for
X\in\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom}\}
.

Efficiency and Correctness
. Each step of
\xrightarrow{\alpha}_{{\tt tr}}
is computable in polynomial time(see the supplementary material). This is based on the fact that readable and visible sets are computable in polynomial time.

The correctness of the trace semantics for a model
X

stems from the fact that it generates only those
X
-extensions which do not have cycles (Lemma
5.1
). The design
of the transitions ensures that the resultant extended trace
does not have a cycle.

6.
DPOR Algorithm for
{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom}

We present our DPOR algorithm, which systematically explores, for any terminating program under the consistency models
X\in\{{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RAt},{\tt RCom}\}
, all traces
\tau_{X}
wrt
X
which can be generated by the trace semantics. Enabled transactions from any of the processes are added to the trace generated so far, and we proceed with the next transaction. For a transaction
t
with a read event, we explore in separate branches, all possible transactions
t^{\prime}
with write events from which
t
can read. Each such branch is a sequence of transactions also called a
schedule
.
There may be transactions
t^{\prime\prime}
which will be added to the trace later in the exploration, from which
t
can also read. Such transactions
t^{\prime\prime}
are called
postponed
wrt
t
; when
t^{\prime\prime}
is added to the trace later, the algorithm will have a branch where
t
can read from
t^{\prime\prime}
. In that branch,
the algorithm reorders transactions in the sequence s.t.
t^{\prime\prime}
and
t
exchange places, and all transactions which are needed for
t^{\prime\prime}
to occur are also
placed before
t^{\prime\prime}
(
CreateSchedule
). All generated schedules will be executed by
RunSchedule
.
The algorithm is uniform across the models,
with the main technical differences being taken care of
by the respective trace semantics which guides the exploration
of traces.

Input:

X\in\{{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt},{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM}\}
, transactional program
\mathcal{P}

1

\textsc{inpProgram}=\mathcal{P}

/*
global variable storing the program */

2

\textsc{ExploreTraces}(X,\tau_{\emptyset},\epsilon)

Algorithm 1

DPOR
(
X
,
\mathcal{P}
)

The main procedure is
\textsc{DPOR}(X,\mathcal{P})
which invokes the procedure

\textsc{ExploreTraces}(X,\tau_{\emptyset},\epsilon)
, to explore all traces of the input program
\mathcal{P}
under model
X
.

ExploreTraces
. This algorithm takes as input, a consistency model
X
, an

X
-extension
\tau_{X}
and an
observation sequence

\pi
.

\pi
is a sequence of events

ev\in\{\mathsf{begin}(t)
,
\mathsf{end}(t)
,
\mathsf{write}(t),

\mathsf{read}(t,t^{\prime})\}
.
Events from the same transaction appear contiguously in
\pi
.
The initial invocation is with the empty trace
\tau_{0}

and observation sequence
\pi=\epsilon
.
The observation sequence is
used to swap transactions
t
having read events with transactions
t^{\prime\prime}
that are
postponed

wrt them.
A Boolean array
locally[~]
of size
|\mathcal{V}|
is reset to

{\tt false}
for all variables at the start of any transaction;
when a write event on
x
takes place in the current transaction,
then
locally[x]
is set to
{\tt true}
.
This signifies the local availability
of an
(x,v)
in the log
. We also maintain a map
currentreads
from variables to transactions :
currentreads[x]=t^{\prime}

means that
x
read from transaction
t^{\prime}
; hence,
all later read events of
x
in the current transaction
t
must read from
t^{\prime}
,
unless
t
writes to
x
between an earlier read (from
t^{\prime}
) and
the next read
1
1

1

This case does apply for
{\tt RCom}
, later reads of x can read
from transaction other than
t^{\prime}
.
.

GetNextEvent
() returns the next event
e
and the last index
i
of
\pi
.

\bullet

If we are at the last event of
\pi
and
it is not
\mathsf{end}(t)

then
GetNextEvent
() returns the next event
e
(read, write or
\mathsf{end}
) and its index from the current transaction
t
.

\bullet
If
e
writes on
x
, set the flag
locally[x]
TRUE to indicate that a local write on
x
is available. Update
\pi{\leftarrow}\pi.e
and recursively call on
ExploreTraces
. Subsequent reads on
x
from
t
will read from
t
.

\bullet
Let
e
be a read event on
x
in transaction
t
.
e
can read from the latest write in
t
if
locally[x]
is true. Otherwise, it reads from the transaction stored in
currentreads[x]
(this is a map from variables to transactions which maintains for each variable, the transaction it can be read from). In this case,

\texttt{Swappable}\!\left(e\right)
is
{\tt false}
, indicating there is no need
to look for a source transaction to read from.
If
currentreads[x]
is empty, then the set
\tt{Sche}
\tt{dules(e)}
is initialized and the Boolean flag
\texttt{Swappable}\!\left(e\right)
is set to
{\tt true}
.

\texttt{Swappable}\!\left(e\right)
indicates that we must find, according to the trace semantics, readable transactions
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{w,x}
where
e
can read from, leading to
\tau^{\prime}_{X}
.
For each such
t^{\prime}
, let
\pi=\zeta\cdot\mathsf{begin}(t)\dots\mathsf{read}(t,t^{\prime})
be the observation sequence obtained
on choosing to read from
t^{\prime}
.
currentreads[x]
is set to
t^{\prime}
and
ExploreTraces
() is called recursively.
If transaction
t^{\prime}
has not yet appeared in
\pi
, the write in
t^{\prime}
is called a
postponed write
.

\bullet
If
e
is
\mathsf{end}(t)
, then we recursively call on
ExploreTraces
after appending
\mathsf{end}(t)
to
\pi
, exploring
other transactions. When the recursive calls return to this point when the last event of
\pi
is
\mathsf{end}(t)
, we do the following.
Corresponding to the last write for each variable in transaction
t
, we call
CreateSchedule
.
This call enables us to pair these writes as potential sources to be read from, for the reads in transactions which are dependent on postponed writes.
This call also creates schedules for such reads which are identified in
CreateSchedule
.
These schedules are then explored when the recursive call to
ExploreTraces
returns.
For a read event
e
in a transaction
t
, each schedule
\beta\in\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e\right)
explores a new source transaction

t^{\prime}
to read from, and extends the trace.

CreateSchedule
.
The inputs to this algorithm are a consistency model
X
, trace
\tau_{X}
wrt
X
, a variable
x
, and
an observation sequence
\pi
whose last element is a
e=\mathsf{end}(t_{2})
event of a transaction

t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathsf{wt},x}
.

CreateSchedule
looks for read events
r
occurring in a transaction
t_{1}
in
\pi

for which
t_{2}
is postponed. Indeed,
(i)
r
must be swappable,
(ii)
t_{1}
must not be
\mathsf{CO}
before
t_{2}
, and (iii)

\tt{\bf{CanRead}(X,r,t_{1},t_{2},\tau_{X})}
must be true (that is,
t_{2}
is readable for
r
according to the trace semantics). We begin with the closest (from
e=\mathsf{end}(t_{2})
) transaction
t_{1}
containing such a read
r
. A schedule
\beta
is created for
r
. The schedule contains
all elements following
\mathsf{end}(t_{1})
in
\pi
from transactions
following
t_{1}
in
\pi
and preceding
t_{2}
wrt
\mathsf{CO}
. The schedule ends with
\mathsf{begin}(t_{2})\dots,\mathsf{end}(t_{2})\mathsf{begin}(t_{1})\dots\mathsf{read}(t_{1},t_{2})
enabling the read
r
in
t_{1}
to read from
t_{2}
. This schedule
is added to
{\tt Schedules}(r)
if it does not contain a
\beta^{\prime}

which has the same set of observations.

RunSchedule
. The inputs are a model
X
, trace
\tau_{X}
, an observation sequence
\pi
and a schedule
\beta
.
The schedule of observations in
\beta
is explored one by one,
by recursively calling itself, and updating the trace. The reads
in the schedule are not swappable, preventing a redundant exploration
for them (schedules where these are swapped with respective writes will
be created by
CreateSchedule
).

7.
An Illustrating Example for
{\tt CCv}
DPOR

We illustrate the DPOR algorithm on Figure
5
.
We have presented all the traces as well as the recursion tree in Figure
6
.
The nodes
n_{0}\dots n_{35}
represent recursive calls to
ExploreTraces
and
RunSchedule
.

n_{0}
is the first call to
ExploreTraces
with an empty trace and observation sequence. The detailed description can be seen in the supplementary material.

From
n_{0}
, consider the case when
ExploreTraces
selects
t1
, making recursive calls to itself after each instruction in
t1
.
The two reads of
t1
each have two sources to read from,
init_{x}
or
t4
, and
init_{y}
or
t2
.
The reads from
t4,t2
will be scheduled later, by a call to
CreateSchedule
, as indicated by the orange arrows.
The last call to
ExploreTraces
in
t1
is made at
\mathsf{end}(t1)

reaching node
n_{1}
.
Next,
ExploreTraces
selects
t2
, and the last recursive call
in
t2
leads to node
n_{5}
, where
t3
is selected.
The read in
t3
has two choices to read from :
t1
and
t2
,
and makes two recursive calls after each choice.
The respective last recursive calls to
ExploreTraces
leading to
n_{9},n_{10}
.

t5
is selected next, leading to nodes

n_{16}
and
n_{17}
. Finally,
t4
is chosen.
t4
has two
sources to read from, namely,
t5
and
t3
, leading to nodes
n_{23}
to
n_{26}
.
This gives traces
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{4}
, and respective observation sequences.
For eg., the observation sequence for
\tau_{3}
is

\mathsf{begin}(t1),\mathsf{read}(t1,init_{x}),\mathsf{read}(t1,init_{y})
,

\mathsf{write}(t1),\mathsf{end}(,t1),\mathsf{begin}(t2),\dots,\mathsf{end}(t2)
,

\mathsf{begin}(t3),\mathsf{read}(t3,t1),\mathsf{write}(t3)
,

\mathsf{end}(t3)
,

\mathsf{begin}(t5)
,

\dots,\mathsf{end}(t5),\mathsf{begin}(t4),\mathsf{read}(t4,t5)
,
\mathsf{write}(t4),\mathsf{end}(t4)
.
The recursive call to
ExploreTraces
from
n_{23},n_{24},n_{25},n_{26}
returns as there are no more transactions, and
CreateSchedule
is called from these since
t4

has write instructions.

Fig. 5.
Program to illustrate DPOR

Fig. 6.
Traces and recursion tree for the program in Figure
5
under
{\tt CCv}
.
The nodes
n_{0}
to
n_{35}
represent recursive calls to Algorithm 2. The black edges come from Algorithm 2, green edges represent
Algorithm 4 running a schedule created by a call to Algorithm 3.
The orange lines denote the pairs of writes and reads, resulting in schedules. The edges are decorated with the transactions executed, and register values read.

From
n_{23}
,
CreateSchedule
is invoked when the recursive call of
ExploreTraces
returns
to the point after
\mathsf{end}(t4)
.
We traverse back in the observation sequence till

\mathsf{begin}(t4)
(line 2 of
CreateSchedule
).
We keep moving back till obtaining the read event
e^{\prime}
of
r1
in
t1
, for which

\texttt{Swappable}\!\left(e^{\prime}\right)
set to
{\tt true}
in
ExploreTraces
.
We check if
t1
happens before
t4
, which it does not, and also check that
t4
is readable for
t1
(
\tt{\bf{CanRead()}}
).
Thus,
t4
and
t1
can be swapped.
The schedule
\beta_{1}
is initialized.
We then check whether the transactions
t2,t3,t5

which happen between
\mathsf{end}(t1)
and
\mathsf{begin}(t4)

should be retained before
t4
in the schedule for
e^{\prime}
.
Since
\neg t2~\mathsf{CO}~t4,\neg t3~\mathsf{CO}~t4
,
the schedule need not contain
t2,t3
, and we skip over them.
However,
t5~\mathsf{CO}~t4
must be retained in the schedule.
So we obtain
\beta_{1}=\mathsf{begin}(t5)\dots\mathsf{end}(t5)\cdot

\mathsf{begin}(t4)

\dots

\mathsf{end}(t4)\mathsf{begin}(t1)

\dots

\mathsf{read}(t1,t4)
in
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime}\right)
.
After this, the call from
CreateSchedule
returns, and we trace back to
n_{16}
and change the source (from the readable set) for
r4
, so
r4=2
and we get the new trace
\tau_{2}
.
The call to
ExploreTraces
at node
n_{24}
returns to the point after
\mathsf{end}(t4)
, after which procedure
CreateSchedule
is invoked. The corresponding read event is again
e^{\prime}
of
r1
in
t1
.
As seen above, we
check whether the transactions
t2,t3,t5

which happen between
\mathsf{end}(t1)
and
\mathsf{begin}(t4)

should be retained before
t4
in the schedule for
e^{\prime}
.
Indeed,
t3~\mathsf{CO}~t4,t2~\mathsf{CO}~t3,t5[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}]t4
.
Hence we
obtain another schedule
\beta_{2}
=
\mathsf{begin}(t2)

\dots\mathsf{end}(t2)

\mathsf{begin}(t3)

\dots

\mathsf{end}(t3)\mathsf{begin}(t5)

\dots

\mathsf{end}(t5)\mathsf{begin}(t4)

\dots\mathsf{end}(t4)\mathsf{begin}(t1)\dots\mathsf{read}(t1,t4)
in
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime}\right)
.
Then we backtrack to
n_{16}
and call

CreateSchedule
at
\mathsf{end}(t5)
which returns an empty schedule since there are no corresponding read events.
Then we backtrack to node
n_{9}
and then to
n_{5}
till we can change the source for
r3
, which reads from
t1
. We continue exploring traces with
ExploreTraces
and obtain
\tau_{3},\tau_{4}
.
We invoke
CreateSchedule
from nodes
n_{25}
and
n_{26}
, however these do not add any more schedules to
e^{\prime}
, since they are equivalent to what has been added already in
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime}\right)
.
From
n_{25}
, we backtrack and reach node
n_{5}
and invoke

CreateSchedule
at
\mathsf{end}(t2)
which creates a schedule

\gamma_{1}
corresponding to the read event
e^{\prime\prime}
of
r2
in
t1
.

\gamma_{1}
=
\mathsf{begin}(t2)\dots\mathsf{end}(t2)\mathsf{begin}(t1)\mathsf{read}(t1,init_{x})

\mathsf{read}(t1,t2)
is added to
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime\prime}\right)
.
We then backtrack till the point
r2=0

and find that
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime\prime}\right)
is non-empty.

RunSchedule
runs the schedule
\gamma_{1}
(shown in green edges
n_{0}-n_{2}-n_{6}
), followed by recursive calls to
ExploreTraces
, resulting in traces
\tau_{5},\tau_{6},\tau_{7},\tau_{8}
.
At node
n_{30}
on return from
ExploreTraces
, we backtrack till the point
r1=0
and find two schedules
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
in
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime}\right)
.
We invoke
RunSchedule
first for
\beta_{1}
,
obtaining traces
\tau_{9},\tau_{10}
ending at nodes
n_{31},n_{32}
. At nodes
n_{31},n_{32}
, we invoke
CreateSchedule
at
\mathsf{end}(t3)
but these will not generate any schedules for the read
r4
in
t4
since
\tt{Swappable}
is
{\tt false}
for this read event. Note that

t3[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}]t4
(along with
t4[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}]t1,t2[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}]t1
) is covered by trace
\tau_{13}
; thus, we optimize on redundant traces by keeping
\tt{Swappable}

{\tt false}
.
We backtrack till
\mathsf{end}(t2)
at
n_{20}
and
invoke
CreateSchedule
corresponding to the write in
t2
, which can be a source for the read in
t1
.
This adds the schedule
\gamma_{2}
=
\mathsf{begin}(t2)\dots\mathsf{end}(t2)

\mathsf{begin}(t1)\mathsf{read}(t1,t4)\mathsf{read}(t1,t2)
to
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e^{\prime\prime}\right)
.
On returning from this call, we backtrack till the read
e^{\prime\prime}
of
r2=0
in
t1
and invoke
RunSchedule
at node
n_{7}
(the green edges
n_{7}-n_{14}-n_{21}
) for
\gamma_{2}
.
After
RunSchedule
finishes
n_{21}
, we invoke
ExploreTraces
and obtain traces
\tau_{11},\tau_{12}
.

At node
n_{34}
on return from
ExploreTraces
, we backtrack till the read event
e^{\prime}
of
r1
in
t1
and invoke
RunSchedule
on the remaining schedule
\beta_{2}
, obtaining trace
\tau_{13}
.

7.1.
Complexity, Soundness, and Completeness of the DPOR algorithm

Theorem 7.1.

The DPOR algorithm for
{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
is sound, complete and optimal.

The proof of Theorem
7.1
is in Appendices
A.2
,

A.3
,
B.2
,
B.3
,
C.2
,
C.3
,
D.2
,
D.3
,
E.2
,
E.3
. Our DPOR algorithm spends polynomial time per explored trace : (1) each schedule has a length bounded by the program length, (2) each schedule we generate corresponds to a part of a trace that has not been generated before and is added to the set of schedules only if it has not been added. Checking if a new schedule is equal to an existing one is done in poly time: arrange all created schedules as a tree and compare from the root, wrt new schedule, going over the
\mathsf{CO}
relations.

Soundness, Optimality and Completeness
. The algorithm is sound : if we initiate Algorithm 2 from
(X,\tau_{\emptyset},\epsilon)
,
all explored traces

\tau_{X}
are s.t.
\tau_{X}\models X
. This follows from the fact that the exploration uses the

\xrightarrow{}_{X{-}{\tt tr}}
relation.
The algorithm is optimal in the sense that, for any two different recursive calls to Algorithm 2 with arguments

(X,\tau^{1}_{X},\pi_{1})
and
(X,\tau^{2}_{X},\pi_{2})
, if
\tau^{1}_{X},\tau^{2}_{X}
are etendible, then
\tau^{1}_{X}\neq\tau^{2}_{X}
.
This follows from (i) each time we run the for loop in line 19 of Algorithm 2, the read event will read from a different source, (ii) in each schedule
\beta\in\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e\right)
in line 22 of Algorithm 2, the event
e
reads from a transaction
t
which is different from all transactions it reads from in line 19 of Algorithm 2, (iii) Any two schedules added to
\texttt{Schedules}\!\left(e\right)
at line 22 of Algorithm 3 will have a read event reading from different transactions.
The algorithm is complete in the sense that, it produces all traces corresponding to terminating runs.

8.
Experiments

We describe the implementation of our optimal DPOR algorithm for
the causal consistency models
{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
as a tool
Tranchecker
.
To the best of our knowledge,
Tranchecker
is the first stateless model checking tool for the causal consistency models
{\tt CC},{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RCom},{\tt RAt}
.

Tranchecker
.

Tranchecker
extends
Nidhugg

(
Abdulla et al. 2015
)
and
works at LLVM IR level accepting a C language program as input.
At runtime,
Tranchecker
controls the exploration of the input program
until it has explored all the traces using the DPOR algorithm.
It can detect user-provided assertion violations by analyzing the generated traces. We conduct all experiments on a Ubuntu 22.04.1 LTS with Intel Core i7-1165G7 and 16 GB RAM.
We evaluate
Tranchecker
on the following categories of benchmarks, as seen below.

Evaluation on Litmus Tests
.

The first set of experiments (Table 1) includes litmus tests.
These litmus tests are non-transactional programs with assertions at end of each program generated using the Herd tool
(
Alglave
et al. 2014
)
.
as a sanity check in tool development.
We generated 7507 litmus tests
and instrumented them into transactional programs.
Given the small number of instructions
per process in the litmus tests
of
(
Alglave
et al. 2014
)
, we group all instructions in a process as a single transaction.
In these litmus tests, the processes execute concurrently, and we check if an assertion is violated or not.
Table
3

describes the outcomes observed on the litmus tests under all five consistency models.
An outcome
\bf{Allow}
in Table
3
means that the program has an execution that violates the assertion under the respective consistency model; otherwise, the outcome is
\bf{Forbid}
. The time taken per model
to evaluate all 7507 tests is atmost 570 seconds.

Evaluation on Classical Transactional Benchmarks
.

The second set (Table
3
) consists of classical benchmark examples from databases on well-known transaction related anomalies

(
Bernardi and
Gotsman 2016
)
,
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
,
(
Beillahi
et al. 2021b
)
and
(
Beillahi
et al. 2021a
)
.
We consider 14 such examples.
While most of them are taken from cited sources, the ones
without a citation in Table
3

are created by us (see Appendix
F.2
).

For each of these examples, we have made three versions (see Appendix

F.1
)
by parameterizing on the number of processes and transactions.
In version 1, we have two to four processes per program and two transactions per process. Version 2 is obtained
allowing each process to have three to four transactions. Version 3
expands version 2 by allowing each program to have four to five processes.
Versions 2,3 serve as a stress test for
Tranchecker
as increasing the number of transactions and
processes increases the number of executions to be explored and the number
of consistent traces.

Tranchecker
took less than four second to finish running all version 1 programs,
about 40
2
2

2

under
{\tt RCom}
consistency it took 160s, where the program
Conflict Violation
took about 85s to generate 551781 traces.
seconds to finish running all
version 2 programs, and about 450 to 1200
3
3

3

under
{\tt RCom}
consistency it took 3900s, where the program
Writeskew
took about 2360s to generate 7726230 traces.
seconds to finish running all version 3 programs
across the models.
All programs in Table
3
are classified as
SAFE
or
UNSAFE
,
depending on whether or not they have executions violating the assertion.
Since programs under
{\tt CC},{\tt RCom}
semantics may exhibit more behaviours than
{\tt CCv},{\tt CM},{\tt RAt}

we observe more
UNSAFE
under
{\tt CC}
and
{\tt RCom}
semantics
while they are
SAFE
under the
{\tt CCv},{\tt CM}
and
{\tt RAt}
semantics.

Evaluation on some Database Applications
.

The third set (Table 2) of benchmarks consists of transactional programs inspired
from six distributed systems and database applications
(
Difallah et al. 2013
)
,
(
Holt et al. 2016
)
,

(
Nair
et al. 2020
)
. For each application,
Tranchecker

verifies the assertions given in these papers on all possible executions. For instance, Auction
(
Nair
et al. 2020
)
is a buggy application where
the highest bid may not win, while Auction-2
(
Nair
et al. 2020
)
is the non buggy version where only the highest bid will win.
Tranchecker
detected the execution leading to the bug in Auction
(
Nair
et al. 2020
)
, and also verified that in all executions of Auction-2, the highest bid wins.
Group is a synthetic application created by us, inspired from whatsapp groups where
we verify that a new user can be added to a whatsapp group only once.

9.
Conclusion

In this paper, we have provided a DPOR algorithm using the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
equivalence for transactional programs under five prominent causal consistency models, and also implemented the same in a tool
Tranchecker
. This is the first tool for stateless model checking of transactional causal consistency models. As future work, we plan to adapt
our algorithm to two of the strongest consistency notions known, namely snapshot isolation(SI) and serializability (SER). The main challenge in handling these two models is that the complexity of checking if a given
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}po}
-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\it{rf}}
trace is SI/SER consistent is an NP-complete problem
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017b
)
. Hence, we need to come up with some
heuristics to have an algorithm which will scale well in practice.
